| 927 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Read the biography. What was her biggest challenge? Today, Old Tock opens a door with bright lights around it. It's the quiz hall! There are two tables with big red buttons. Old Tock is the quiz host, and Professor Nib keeps the score. Team Sun is Haru and Riko. Team Moon is Kiko and Birimo. Old Tock rings a bell. Ding! Here is the first question. When was the first airplane flight? Say it with me. When was the first airplane flight? Super! Haru presses the button. Buzz! He says, It was in 1903. Old Tock says, That's right! Let's read the quiz cards. "History Quiz Cards. Question one: When was the first airplane flight? Answer: In 1903. The Wright brothers flew for about twelve seconds. Question two: Who invented the telephone? Answer: Alexander Graham Bell, in 1876. Question three: When did Japan's first railway open? Answer: In 1872, between Shimbashi and Yokohama. Question four: When did people first walk on the moon? Answer: In 1969. Bonus question: When were the Tokyo Olympics held? Answer: In 1964." How long did the first flight last? About twelve seconds. Which came first, the telephone or the airplane? The telephone came first. It was invented in eighteen seventy-six. Kiko answers the moon question, and Birimo answers the bonus question. At the end, both teams have the same score. It's a tie! Professor Nib says, Everyone wins today, because everyone learned something new. Now, phonics. Let's review. Invention. Change invent into a noun with tion. 1903. We say nineteen oh three. Structure. Struct means build, so a structure is something that is built. Historian. Ian means a person, so a historian studies history, like Professor Nib. Airport. Port means harbor, so an airport is a harbor for airplanes. Next time, we write our own hero reports. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Read the biography. What was her biggest challenge? Today, Old Tock opens a door with bright lights around it. Old Tock rings a bell. Ding! Here is the first question. When was the first airplane flight? Say it with me. When was the first airplane flight? Super! Haru presses the button. Buzz! He says, It was in 1903. Old Tock says, That's right! Let's read the quiz cards. "History Quiz Cards. Question one: When was the first airplane flight? Answer: In 1903. The Wright brothers flew for about twelve seconds. Question two: Who invented the telephone? Answer: Alexander Graham Bell, in 1876. Question three: When did Japan's first railway open? Answer: In 1872, between Shimbashi and Yokohama. Question four: When did people first walk on the moon? Answer: In 1969. Bonus question: When were the first Tokyo Olympics held? Answer: In 1964." How long did the first flight last? About twelve seconds. Which came first, the telephone or the airplane? The telephone came first. It was invented in eighteen seventy-six. Kiko answers the telephone question, Riko answers the moon question, and Birimo answers the bonus question. At the end, both teams have the same score. It's a tie! Professor Nib says, Everyone wins today, because everyone learned something new. Now, phonics. Let's review. Invention. Invent becomes a noun. Say the end softly, shun. 1903. We say nineteen oh three. Structure. Struct means build, so a structure is something that is built. Historian. Ian means a person, so a historian studies history, like Professor Nib. Airport. Port means harbor, so an airport is a harbor for airplanes. | 928 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, When was the first airplane flight? Today, Old Tock takes us to a quiet writing room. There are wooden desks and old pens. Professor Nib climbs onto a desk and says, Squeak! Today, you will write a hero report. It should be about eighty words. First, write who your hero is. Then, give two reasons. Kiko writes about her grandma. My hero is my grandma. She was born in 1950. I respect her because she is always kind. Say it with me. My hero is my grandma. She was born in 1950. I respect her because she is always kind. Lovely! Haru says, Nineteen fifty? That's the year of our town's big festival! Let's read Kiko's report. "My Hero, by Kiko. My hero is my grandma. She was born in 1950 in a small town by the sea. When she was young, she worked at a bakery. Now she is busy, but she always has time for me. I respect her because she is always kind. For example, she makes soup for our neighbors when they are sick. She also taught me how to cook. I want to be warm and kind like my grandma." What did Kiko's grandma teach her? How to cook. Is this a fact or an opinion? She is always kind. It's an opinion, but Kiko gives a good example. Then Kiko asks, Can you check my report? Riko reads it and says, Sure. Your reasons are clear! Now, phonics. Let's review. Achievement. Change achieve into a noun with ment. 1850. We read it in two pairs, eighteen fifty. Instructor. Struct means build, and an instructor is a person who teaches. Journalist. Ist means a person, so a journalist writes the news. Passport. Port means harbor. Next time, we take a goodbye walk through the Corridor of Time. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, When was the first airplane flight? Today, Old Tock takes us to a quiet writing room. | 929 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My hero is my grandma. She was born in 1950. I respect her because she is always kind. Today, Old Tock says, Tick, tock! Haru looks up and says, People used to live in this castle. It was built long ago. Say it with me. People used to live in this castle. It was built long ago. Perfect! Next, Kiko points to a clay pot and says, This pot was made about 5,000 years ago. Birimo says, Wow, it's so old! Then we pass the Time Theater. Riko says, When we arrived, the show had already started. Let's not be late this time! Let's read the notes on our Time Map. "Our Time Map Notes. At the old road door, we learned that people used to walk to faraway towns. The castle was built about 400 years ago, and people used to live there. An old ship was found under the sea, and a clay pot was made about 5,000 years ago. At the Time Theater, we were late, and the show had already started! After that, we met great people and everyday heroes." What did people use to do on the old road? They used to walk to faraway towns. What had happened when we got to the Time Theater? | 930 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, People used to live in this castle. It was built long ago. Today is the World 31 History Festival! The great hall is full of lights, music, and timelines.Haru shows a picture of his grandpa's bike shop. Kiko shares her hero report, and Riko reads about Marie Curie. Birimo plays Old Tock and swings his arm like a pendulum. Then the real Old Tock says, Long ago, people walked to faraway towns, wrote letters, and built castles. They had problems and big dreams, just like you. History is the story of people like us. Say it with me. History is the story of people like us. Wonderful! Let's read the festival poster. "Welcome to the World 31 History Festival! Walk through the great hall and visit every stand. You can see timelines of old roads, castles, and trains. You can read hero reports about famous people and everyday heroes. You can also join a history quiz show with Old Tock. Remember, people in the past were children once, too. They played, learned, made mistakes, and tried again. Thank you for visiting the Corridor of Time!" What can you do at the festival? You can see timelines, read hero reports, and join a quiz show. Is this a fact or an opinion? History is the story of people like us. It's an opinion, and a beautiful one! Suddenly, the Time Map shines. Click! The sixth golden gear drops in. Congratulations! All six gears are shining! Old Tock opens the last door. Bright sunlight pours in. It's the Desert of the Sun! Now, phonics. Let's review. Celebration. Change celebrate into a noun with tion. The 21st century. We live in it now. Construction. Struct means build. Scientist. Ist means a person. Spaceport. Port means harbor. See you in the Desert of the Sun! | Hello, everyone! | 932 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, It hardly ever rains here. Today, Fen leads us to a little camp between two sand dunes. The afternoon sun is burning, and the sand is too hot to touch. Haru looks at the thermometer. Wow! It's forty-two degrees! We sit in the shade of the tent and drink lots of water. Then the sun goes down. The sky turns orange, pink, and purple. Soon, thousands of stars come out, but we start to shiver. Brr! Now we need jackets and blankets! Fen says, In the desert, it's very hot during the day, but cold at night. Say it with me. It's very hot during the day, but cold at night. Great! Riko asks, Why does it get so cold? Fen explains. The air here is very dry, and there are no clouds. Clouds and wet air work like a blanket. Without them, the heat of the day escapes into the sky. Before bed, Haru writes in his diary. Let's read it. "Day One in the Desert. This afternoon, it was over forty degrees. My water bottle felt like hot tea! We stayed in the shade and didn't run around. At sunset, everything changed. The wind became cool, and the temperature dropped very fast. Fen says desert nights can get close to zero degrees. Now I'm wearing my jacket, two pairs of socks, and a hat. But the stars are amazing. I have never seen so many stars in my life!" How hot was it in the afternoon? Over forty degrees. Why is Haru wearing two pairs of socks? Because the night is very cold. Now, phonics. Geo means the earth, and today we meet some geo people. Geographer. Add e, r to geography, and it means a person who studies geography. Geologist. A geologist studies rocks and the ground. Geoscientist. A geoscientist studies the whole Earth, its land, water, and air. Next time, we will find water in the middle of the desert! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, It hardly ever rains here. Today, Fen leads us to a little camp between two sand dunes. The afternoon sun is burning, and the sand is too hot to touch. Haru looks at the thermometer. Wow! It's forty-two degrees! We sit in the shade of the tent and drink lots of water. Then the sun goes down. The sky turns orange, pink, and purple. Soon, thousands of stars come out, but we start to shiver. Brr! Now we need jackets and blankets! Fen says, In the desert, it's very hot during the day, but cold at night. Say it with me. It's very hot during the day, but cold at night. Great! Riko asks, Why does it get so cold? Fen explains. The air here is very dry, and there are no clouds. Clouds and wet air work like a blanket. Without them, the heat of the day escapes into the sky. Before bed, Haru writes in his diary. Let's read it. "Day One in the Desert. This afternoon, it was over forty degrees. My water bottle felt like hot tea! We stayed in the shade and didn't run around. At sunset, everything changed. The wind became cool, and the temperature dropped very fast. Fen says desert nights can get close to zero degrees. Now I'm wearing my jacket, two pairs of socks, and a hat. But the stars are amazing. I have never seen so many stars in my life!" How hot was it in the afternoon? Over forty degrees. Why is Haru wearing two pairs of socks? Because the night is very cold. Now, phonics. Geo means the earth, and today we meet some geo people. Geographer. Take the y off geography and add e, r. It means a person who studies geography. Geologist. A geologist studies rocks and the ground. Geoscientist. A geoscientist studies the whole Earth, its land, water, and air. Next time, we will find water in the middle of the desert! | objective | geography + er is not geographer; the y drops before e, r |

| 934 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, An oasis is a place where water comes out. Today, a strong wind is blowing sand everywhere, so we stay inside Fen's big tent. Fen rolls out a huge map of the world. It's covered in colors: yellow, green, white, and dark green. Fen says, This is my climate map. Climate means the usual weather in a place over many years. Haru points to the dark green part. What's the weather like here? Fen says, That's the rainforest. It's hot and rainy all year. Some places are rainy all year. Say it with me. Some places are rainy all year. Wonderful! Kiko finds Japan. It's light green. Fen says, Some places have four seasons, like Japan. Let's read the key on the map. "Climate Map Key. Dark green: rainforest. It is hot and rainy all year, and tall trees grow very close together. The Amazon rainforest in South America is the largest one. Yellow: desert. It is dry all year. Some deserts, like the Sahara, are hot. Others, like the Gobi in Asia, have very cold winters. Light green: four seasons. Summers are warm, winters are cool or cold, and it rains in many months. White: polar. It is freezing all year, and the land is covered with ice and snow." What's the weather like in the rainforest? It's hot and rainy all year. Which desert has very cold winters? | 936 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The Sahara is located in North Africa. It's hot and dry. Today, we hear bells. Ding, ding, ding. A long line of camels is walking over the dunes. It's a caravan! At the front, there is a big old camel with a gentle smile. He kneels down slowly and says, Good morning, little travelers. I'm Humphrey. I lead this caravan. Hop on! We climb onto the camels. Whoa! They stand up back legs first, and we almost fall off! Later, Haru asks, Humphrey, aren't you thirsty? Humphrey smiles. Not yet. Camels can go many days without water. Say it with me. Camels can go many days without water. Great! Kiko asks, Is there water in your hump? Humphrey shakes his head. Our humps store fat, not water. Humphrey carries a fact card in his saddle bag. Let's read it. "Camel Fact Card. Camels are amazing desert travelers. They can go about a week or more without water. When they finally find water, they can drink about one hundred liters at one time! Their humps store fat, not water. Long eyelashes protect their eyes, and they can close their noses to keep sand out. Their wide feet don't sink in soft sand. They also have tough mouths, so they can eat thorny desert plants. Some people call camels the ships of the desert." How much water can a camel drink at one time? About one hundred liters. What do camels' humps store? Fat. Is this a fact or an opinion? Camels are the best animals in the world. It's an opinion, but Humphrey likes it! Now, phonics. Today's ending is a, l. Al turns a word into a describing word. Nature, natural. Drop the e, and the sound changes from nay to na. Nation, national. Nay becomes na again. Tropic, tropical. Tropical places are hot and wet. Next time, we will watch desert animals, and Fen will tell us the secret of his big ears! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The Sahara is located in North Africa. It's hot and dry. Heat leaves my body through them. Desert foxes have big ears so that they can stay cool. So that（～できるように） means for this reason, or to make something possible. Say it with me. Desert foxes have big ears so that they can stay cool. Perfect! Fen turns his ears this way and that way. They help me hear bugs moving under the sand, too. Dinner! Haru asks, Why are your feet so furry? Fen says, So that I can walk on hot sand. Let's read a page from Fen's animal notebook. "How Desert Animals Survive. I'm a fennec fox, the smallest fox in the world. My huge ears let heat out of my body so that I can stay cool. They also help me hear insects under the sand. My feet are covered with fur so that I can walk on hot sand. During the day, I sleep in a cool burrow under the ground. At night, when the air is cooler, I go out to hunt. My friend Humphrey has long eyelashes so that sand can't get into his eyes." Why does Fen have furry feet? So that he can walk on hot sand. When does Fen hunt? At night. Now, phonics. Let's add a, l again. Coast, coastal. A coastal town is a town by the sea. Season, seasonal. Seasonal fruit comes only in one season, like watermelon in summer. Center, central. | 940 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Penguins have thick feathers to keep warm. Today is the Adaptation Fair at the oasis! Fen explains. Adaptation means the special body parts and habits that help an animal live in its home. Every table has a poster and a quiz. Riko's poster is about Fen and his big ears. Birimo's poster shows a giant cactus. Then Haru holds up his poster with a picture hidden under a cloth. He says, This animal has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Say it with me. This animal has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Wonderful! Kiko shouts, What animal is it? Is it a camel? Behind us, someone laughs. It's Humphrey! Yes, it's me! Let's read Haru's quiz poster. "Guess the Animal! This animal lives in hot, dry deserts. It has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Its long eyelashes keep sand out of its eyes, and it can close its nose in a sandstorm. It can go many days without water, and its hump stores fat, not water. Its tough mouth can even eat thorny plants. Some people call it the ship of the desert. Can you guess? Hint: he leads our caravan!" What helps this animal walk on soft sand? Its wide feet. What does its hump store? Fat. Humphrey bows his long neck. What a wonderful fair! Fen's ears turn pink with joy. Then the Sun Compass glows again. Click! The second sun crystal drops into its slot. Now we have two crystals! Now, phonics. Let's review. Natural. Nature becomes natural with a, l. Seasonal. Season plus a, l. Global. Drop the e from globe and add a, l. And one geo word. Geopark, a park where we learn about the earth. Next time, we will learn north, south, east, and west, and read a map with a compass! | Hello, everyone! Birimo's poster shows a giant cactus. Then Haru holds up his poster with a picture hidden under a cloth. He says, This animal has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Say it with me. This animal has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Wonderful! Kiko shouts, What animal is it? Is it a camel? Behind us, someone laughs. It's Humphrey! He says, Yes, it's me! Let's read Haru's quiz poster. "Guess the Animal! This animal lives in hot, dry deserts. It has wide feet so that it can walk on soft sand. Its long eyelashes keep sand out of its eyes, and it can close its nose in a sandstorm. It can go many days without water, and its hump stores fat, not water. Its tough mouth can even eat thorny plants. Some people call it the ship of the desert. Can you guess? Hint: he leads our caravan!" What helps this animal walk on soft sand? Its wide feet. What does its hump store? Fat. Humphrey bows his long neck. What a wonderful fair! Fen's ears turn pink with joy. Then the Sun Compass glows again. Click! The second sun crystal drops into its slot. Now we have two crystals! Now, phonics. Let's review. Natural. Nature becomes natural with a, l. Seasonal. Season plus a, l. Global. Drop the e from globe and add a, l. And one geo word. Geopark, a park where we learn about the earth. Next time, we will learn north, south, east, and west, and read a map with a compass! | A+C | Humphrey's line had no speaker tag after the narrator's 'It's Humphrey!' |

| 943 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The mountains are located in the north. Today, Fen rolls a giant globe into the tent. It's as tall as Birimo! Fen gives it a push, and the whole world spins round and round. Blue, green, brown, white. Stop! Look at all this land. These huge pieces of land are called continents. Let's count them together. Asia, Africa, North America, South America, Antarctica, Europe, and Australia. There are seven continents. Say it with me. There are seven continents. Perfect! Asia is the largest continent. Haru finds Japan. It's a small island country to the east of Asia. Kiko asks, How many oceans are there? Fen says, There are five. The Pacific is the largest. Let's read the little guidebook that comes with the globe. "Our Blue and Green Planet. There are seven continents and five oceans on Earth. The oceans are the Pacific, the Atlantic, the Indian, the Southern, and the Arctic. The Pacific Ocean is so big that all the continents could fit inside it. Africa is home to the Sahara, the largest hot desert in the world. Antarctica is covered with ice, but it's a desert, too, because very little snow or rain falls there. In fact, it's the largest desert on Earth!" Which ocean is the largest? The Pacific. Why is Antarctica a desert? Because very little snow or rain falls there. Now, phonics. W, a, r, d means toward. It sounds like word, but we spell it w, a, r, d. Northward. Southward. Eastward. Westward. If you fly eastward from Japan, you cross the Pacific Ocean and reach North America. If you fly southward, you reach Australia. Next time, Fen has a quiz for us. Where in the world is it? | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The mountains are located in the north. There are seven continents. Perfect! Asia is the largest continent. Haru finds Japan. It's a small island country in East Asia. Kiko asks, How many oceans are there? Fen says, There are five. The Pacific is the largest. Let's read the little guidebook that comes with the globe. "Our Blue and Green Planet. There are seven continents and five oceans on Earth. Africa is home to the Sahara, the largest hot desert in the world. Antarctica is covered with ice, but it's a desert, too, because very little snow or rain falls there. In fact, it's the largest desert on Earth!" Which ocean is the largest? The Pacific. Why is Antarctica a desert? Because very little snow or rain falls there. Now, phonics. W, a, r, d means toward. It sounds like word, but we spell it w, a, r, d. Northward. Southward. Eastward. Westward. If you fly eastward from Japan, you cross the Pacific Ocean and reach North America. If you fly southward, you reach Australia. Next time, Fen has a quiz for us. | 946 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Go north for two kilometers. You'll see the well. Today, we walk to a little white building on a hill. There's a spinning wind cup on the roof and a big thermometer by the door. It's a weather station! A woman with sandy boots and a big smile opens the door. Welcome! I'm Dr. Mira Sands. I study desert weather, and I love graphs! Dr. Mira points to a poster on the wall. A red line goes up and down across it. The graph shows the temperature in July. Say it with me. The graph shows the temperature in July. Wonderful! Look at the red line. It goes up and down like sand dunes. What was the highest temperature? It was forty-six degrees! Let's read Dr. Mira's weather notes. "July Weather Notes. The graph shows the highest temperature on each day in July. Every day was hotter than forty degrees. The hottest day was July twentieth. It was forty-six degrees, and even the lizards stayed in the shade! The coolest day was July third, when clouds covered the sky. It never rained this month. Tip: In July, drink lots of water and stay inside in the afternoon." What was the hottest day? July twentieth. Why was July third cooler? Because clouds covered the sky. Now, phonics. Let's read big numbers. Dr. Mira's station is three hundred fifty meters above the sea. Three, five, zero. Three hundred fifty. She has four thousand photos of clouds. Four, comma, zero, zero, zero. Four thousand. Her weather balloon flies higher than twelve thousand meters. One, two, comma, zero, zero, zero. Twelve thousand. And sixty thousand people read her weather report every month. Six, zero, comma, zero, zero, zero. Sixty thousand. Remember, no s on hundred or thousand! Next time, we will look at a graph of visitors to a desert park. | Hello, everyone! | 951 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This graph shows the rain in our desert town. As you can see, it hardly ever rains in summer. Today, Fen leads us across the hot sand to Palm Village. In the middle of the village, there is an old stone well. A long line of people waits beside it with buckets and clay jars. An old woman slowly pulls up the rope. Splash! Cool, clear water fills her bucket. Nobody spills a drop. Fen's big ears twitch. He whispers, Here, every drop counts. Water is precious in the desert. Say it with me. Water is precious in the desert. Wonderful! Precious means very important and hard to get, like treasure. Riko asks, Why is water so precious? Fen says, It hardly ever rains here. Let's read the sign next to the well. "Welcome to the Palm Village Well. This well is very old and very deep. The water comes from under the ground, far below the sand. Our village gets less than 100 millimeters of rain a year, so this well is our life. Please take only what you need. Close the lid after you use the well, so that sand and animals stay out. Thank you for helping us. Water is precious, so let's not waste a single drop!" Where does the water come from? From under the ground. Why should you close the lid? So that sand and animals stay out. Is this a fact or an opinion? Water is precious. It's an opinion, but in the desert, everyone shares it! Now, phonics. Today's ending is a, g, e. It sounds like idge, but we spell it a, g, e. Average. Percentage. Storage. Shortage. Storage is a place to keep things, like a big water tank. A shortage means there is not enough. When the rain doesn't come, the village has a water shortage. The hot sun turns the water into vapor, and it goes up into the air. Because of the heat, the lake became smaller. Say it with me. Because of the heat, the lake became smaller. Great! Because of comes before a thing, like the heat or the rain. Let's read Dr. Mira's notebook. "Mirror Lake Notes. Ten years ago, Mirror Lake was about two kilometers wide. Now, it is less than one kilometer wide. Why? First, the summers here have become hotter. Because of the heat, a lot of water goes up into the air every day. Second, it hardly ever rained this year. Third, more and more farms take water from the lake. Because of the dry weather, many plants on the shore turned brown. The fish and birds have fewer places to live now. We must take care of this lake." How wide was the lake ten years ago? About two kilometers. Give one reason why the lake became smaller. | 953 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Because of the heat, the lake became smaller. Today, Fen takes us over a tall sand dune. At the top, we stop and stare. Below us, there is a green valley full of trees, fields, and flowers! Birds are singing everywhere. Fen says, Welcome to Green Valley. Forty years ago, this place was brown and dusty. Then the people had an idea. People planted trees. As a result, the village became green again. Say it with me. People planted trees. As a result, the village became green again. Perfect! As a result tells us what happened next because of something. Let's read the story board in the village square. "The Story of Green Valley. Forty years ago, Green Valley was dry and brown. Strong winds blew sand into the fields, and the crops died. Many families had to leave. Then the villagers made a plan. They planted long rows of trees around the village. The trees stopped the wind and gave shade. Their roots held the soil in place. As a result, the fields stayed soft and wet, and the crops grew again. Then the birds came back, too. Today, Green Valley is green again!" Why did the crops die? Because of strong winds that blew sand into the fields. What happened after the trees grew? The fields stayed soft and wet, and the crops grew again. Green Valley is a story village, but this happens in the real world, too. In Africa, many countries are planting a Great Green Wall of trees across the Sahel, south of the Sahara, to stop the desert from spreading. Now, phonics. Today's ending is e, a, s, e. It sounds like eess. Increase. Decrease. Release. When the number of trees increases, it goes up. When it decreases, it goes down. Release means let something go. Release the bird, and it flies free! | 955 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We use about 200 liters of water a day. Today is a big day. We are presenting our Save Water Campaign in the square of Palm Village. Fen, Dr. Mira, and many villagers sit on colorful rugs in the shade of the palm trees. Kiko holds up our poster. It has a big blue drop on it. Haru steps forward and says, We can save water by turning off the tap. Say it with me. We can save water by turning off the tap. Wonderful! By plus ing tells us how we do it. Riko asks, How else can we save water? Birimo says, We can take shorter showers. Let's read our campaign poster. "Every Drop Counts! Join Our Save Water Campaign. One: Turn off the tap while you brush your teeth. You can save about 6 liters every 30 seconds. Two: Take shorter showers. One minute less saves about 12 liters. Three: Use the water from washing vegetables to water the plants. Four: Collect rainwater on rainy days. Five: If you see a leaky tap, tell an adult. Our goal is to cut our water use by one quarter. Small actions add up. Let's do it together!" How much water can you save with a shower one minute shorter? About 12 liters. What should you do if you see a leaky tap? Tell an adult. The villagers clap and cheer. Fen's big ears stand straight up. Then the Sun Compass begins to glow. Click! The fifth sun crystal clicks into place! Only one slot is empty now. Now, phonics. Let's review. Average. The ending a, g, e sounds like idge. Temperature. The ending u, r, e sounds like cher. Increase. E, a, s, e sounds like eess. Amount. O, u, n, t. One quarter. One, slash, four. That means one of four equal parts. Next time, we read a desert story about a girl who found water. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We use about 200 liters of water a day. Today is a big day. We are presenting our Save Water Campaign in the square of Palm Village. Fen, Dr. Mira, and many villagers sit on colorful rugs in the shade of the palm trees. Kiko holds up our poster. It has a big blue drop on it. Haru steps forward and says, We can save water by turning off the tap. Say it with me. We can save water by turning off the tap. Wonderful! By plus ing tells us how we do it. Riko asks, How else can we save water? Birimo says, We can take shorter showers. Let's read our campaign poster. "Every Drop Counts! Join Our Save Water Campaign. One: Turn off the tap while you brush your teeth. You can save about 6 liters every 30 seconds. Two: Take shorter showers. One minute less saves about 12 liters. Three: Use the water from washing vegetables to water the plants. Four: Collect rainwater on rainy days. Five: If you see a leaky tap, tell an adult. Our goal is to cut our water use by one quarter. Small actions add up. Let's do it together!" How much water can you save if your shower is one minute shorter? About 12 liters. What should you do if you see a leaky tap? Tell an adult. The villagers clap and cheer. Fen's big ears stand straight up. Then the Sun Compass begins to glow. Click! The fifth sun crystal clicks into place! Only one slot is empty now. Now, phonics. Let's review. Average ends like bridge. Temperature ends like picture. Increase ends like geese. Amount ends like count. One quarter. One, slash, four. That means one of four equal parts. Next time, we read a desert story about a girl who found water. | A+B+C | awkward 'with a shower one minute shorter' (A+C); invented respellings idge / cher / eess replaced by real words (B+C) |

| 956 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We can save water by turning off the tap. Tonight, we sit around a campfire under the stars. Fen pulls a small, sandy book out of his bag. He says, Everyone in the desert knows this story. Remember, it's a made-up story. Lina is not a real girl, but her story is like many true stories from dry places. Ms. Emma opens the book and says, Read the story of a girl who found water. Say it with me. Read the story of a girl who found water. Very good! Let's read it together. "Lina lived in a small village in the eastern part of a dry valley. It hardly ever rained there, and the old well was almost empty. Every morning, Lina walked for an hour to get water. One day at sunrise, she saw something strange at the dry riverbed. Green plants were growing there, and little birds were hopping around them. But there was no water anywhere. Lina thought, Plants need water. Maybe there is water under the ground! She ran back and told the villagers. They dug and dug. At last, cool water came up. Because of Lina's sharp eyes, the village had water again." Why did Lina think there was water? Because she saw green plants and birds. What did the villagers do? They dug, and they found water. What is the lesson of the story? Look carefully, and you can find answers. Now, phonics. Let's review. Geology. Geo means earth, and logy means study. Geologists study rocks and the ground. Original. Origin plus a, l. Eastern. East plus e, r, n. Lina's village was in the eastern part of the valley. One third. One, slash, three. One of three equal parts. Moisture. The plants found moisture under the sand. Next time, it's the Geography Quiz Rally around the oasis! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We can save water by turning off the tap. Tonight, we sit around a campfire under the stars. | 959 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The graph shows the number of trees in the village. It increased from 100 to 900. Today, Humphrey the old camel leads our farewell caravan. Bells jingle as we sway across the golden dunes. First, we pass our old camp, where the days were hot and the nights were cold. Humphrey turns his long neck and winks. Do camels store water in their humps? Birimo answers, No, you store fat! Haru says, Camels have humps so that they can live without food. Then we stop at Dr. Mira's weather station. Riko points to the thermometer graph. The graph shows how hot it is. Say it with me. Camels have humps so that they can live without food. The graph shows how hot it is. Wonderful! Let's read Kiko's desert journal. "My Desert Journal. We came to the Desert of the Sun through the Corridor of Time. It hardly ever rains here, so water is precious. At the oasis, water comes up from under the ground, and date palms grow there. Fen has big ears so that he can stay cool. A cactus stores water in its thick stem. Mirror Lake became smaller because of the heat. In Green Valley, people planted trees. As a result, the village became green again. I will miss this place!" Why does Fen have big ears? So that he can stay cool. What happened in Green Valley? People planted trees, and the village became green again. Fen gives each of us a round rock. Crack! Inside, it sparkles with crystals. It's a geode! Now, phonics. Let's review. Geode. Geo means earth. Coastal. Coast plus a, l. Northwest. North comes first. Two point five. Two, dot, five. We say point for the dot. Count. O, u, n, t. Let's count our sun crystals. One, two, three, four, five! Next time, it's the World 32 Earth Festival! | Hello, everyone! | 960 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Camels have humps so that they can live without food. The graph shows how hot it is. Today is the World 32 Earth Festival at the oasis! Lanterns hang from the palm trees, and drums go boom, boom! Haru, Kiko, and Riko share their maps, posters, and reports. Fen, Humphrey, and Dr. Mira sit in the front row. Then Fen stands up and says, Hot deserts, cold deserts, rainforests, and icy lands. Every place is different. Every place on Earth has its own amazing story. Say it with me. Every place on Earth has its own amazing story. Wonderful! Let's read the festival poster. "Welcome to the World 32 Earth Festival! Visit every stand around the oasis. At the climate stand, you can find out why it hardly ever rains in the desert. At the animal stand, you can learn why camels have wide feet. At the graph stand, you can read reports about water and trees. Don't miss the Save Water Corner! Learn how you can save water by turning off the tap. Thank you for exploring the Desert of the Sun with us!" What can you learn at the animal stand? Why camels have wide feet. Is this a fact or an opinion? Every place on Earth has its own amazing story. It's an opinion, and a wonderful one! Suddenly, the Sun Compass shines like the sun itself. Click! The sixth sun crystal drops in. Congratulations! All six sun crystals are shining! The needle spins, then points to the sea. Far away, we see smoke rising from a volcano. It's the Island of Fire! Now, phonics. Let's review. Geography. Geo means earth. Global. Globe plus a, l. Northern. North plus e, r, n. Three million. Three, then two groups of zero zero zero. No s on million. Average. A, g, e sounds like idge. Goodbye, Fen!
